					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom from 1902 to 1905</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the politician. For the steel manufacturer, see <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour,_1st_Baron_Riverdale" title="Arthur Balfour, 1st Baron Riverdale">Arthur Balfour, 1st Baron Riverdale</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Lord Balfour" redirects here. For the titles in the peerages of Scotland, Ireland, and the United Kingdom, see <a href="/wiki/Baron_Balfour_of_Glenawley" title="Baron Balfour of Glenawley">Baron Balfour of Glenawley</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baron_Balfour_of_Inchrye" title="Baron Balfour of Inchrye">Baron Balfour of Inchrye</a>, <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Balfour" title="Earl of Balfour">Earl of Balfour</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Balfour_of_Burleigh" title="Lord Balfour of Burleigh">Lord Balfour of Burleigh</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:#eee;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em;">Member of Parliament<br />for <a 1885</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded </td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Arthur James Balfour</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1848-07-25</span>)</span>25 July 1848<br /><a href="/wiki/Whittingehame_House" class="mw-redirect" title="Whittingehame House">Whittingehame House</a>, East&#160;Lothian, Scotland</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">19 March 1930<span style="display:none">(1930-03-19)</span> (aged&#160;81)<br /><a href="/wiki/Woking" title="Woking">Woking</a>, Surrey, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label">Whittingehame Church, Whittingehame</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Parent</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/James_Maitland_Balfour" title="James Maitland Balfour">James Maitland Balfour</a> (father)</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma mater</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge" title="Trinity College, Cambridge">Trinity College, Cambridge</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Arthur Balfour&#39;s signature"><img alt="Cursive signature in ink" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg/128px-Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="21" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg/192px-Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg/256px-Arthur_Balfour_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="261" data-file-height="43" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Arthur James Balfour, 1st Earl of Balfour</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_British_Academy" title="Fellow of the British Academy">FBA</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Deputy_Lieutenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy Lieutenant">DL</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span>-<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/ɔːr/: &#39;ar&#39; in &#39;war&#39;">ɔːr</span></span>/</a></span></span>,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <span style="font-size:85%;">traditionally <a href="/wiki/Scottish_English" title="Scottish English">Scottish</a></span> <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/əl/: &#39;le&#39; in &#39;bottle&#39;">əl</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/ʊər/: &#39;our&#39; in &#39;tour&#39;">ʊər</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-glasgow.ac.uk_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-glasgow.ac.uk-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> 25 July 1848&#160;&#8211;&#32;19 March 1930), also known as <b>Lord Balfour</b>, was a British statesman and <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1902 to 1905. As <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">foreign secretary</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_George_ministry" title="Lloyd George ministry">Lloyd George ministry</a>, he issued the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1917" class="mw-redirect" title="Balfour Declaration of 1917">Balfour Declaration of 1917</a> on behalf of the cabinet, which supported a "home for the Jewish people" in Palestine.
</p><p>Entering Parliament in <a href="/wiki/1874_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1874 United Kingdom general election">1874</a>, Balfour achieved prominence as <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a>, in which position he suppressed agrarian unrest whilst taking measures against <a href="/wiki/Absentee_landlord" title="Absentee landlord">absentee landlords</a>. He opposed <a href="/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule_movement" title="Irish Home Rule movement">Irish Home Rule</a>, saying there could be no half-way house between Ireland remaining within the United Kingdom or becoming independent. From 1891 he led the Conservative Party in the House of Commons, serving under his uncle, <a href="/wiki/Lord_Salisbury" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Salisbury">Lord Salisbury</a>, whose government won large majorities in <a href="/wiki/1895_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1895 United Kingdom general election">1895</a> and <a href="/wiki/1900_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1900 United Kingdom general election">1900</a>. An esteemed debater, he was bored by the mundane tasks of party management.
</p><p>In July 1902, he succeeded his uncle as prime minister. In domestic policy he passed the <a href="/wiki/Land_Purchase_(Ireland)_Act_1903" class="mw-redirect" title="Land Purchase (Ireland) Act 1903">Land Purchase (Ireland) Act 1903</a>, which bought out most of the Anglo-Irish land owners. The <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1902" title="Education Act 1902">Education Act 1902</a> had a major long-term impact in modernising the school system in England and Wales and provided financial support for schools operated by the Church of England and by the Catholic Church. <a href="/wiki/Nonconformist_(Protestantism)" title="Nonconformist (Protestantism)">Nonconformists</a> were outraged and mobilised their voters, but were unable to reverse it. In foreign and defence policy, he oversaw reform of British defence policy and supported <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher,_1st_Baron_Fisher" title="John Fisher, 1st Baron Fisher">Jackie Fisher</a>'s naval innovations. He secured the <a href="/wiki/Entente_Cordiale" title="Entente Cordiale">Entente Cordiale</a> with France, an alliance that ended centuries of <a href="/wiki/Anglo-French_Wars" title="Anglo-French Wars">intermittent conflict</a> between the two states and their predecessors. He cautiously embraced <a href="/wiki/Imperial_preference" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial preference">imperial preference</a> as championed by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a>, but resignations from the Cabinet over the abandonment of free trade left his party divided. He also suffered from public anger at the later stages of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Boer War</a> (counter-insurgency warfare characterised as "methods of barbarism") and the importation of Chinese labour to South Africa ("Chinese slavery"). He resigned as prime minister in December 1905 and the following month the Conservatives suffered a landslide defeat at the <a href="/wiki/1906_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1906 United Kingdom general election">1906 election</a>, in which he lost his own seat. He soon re-entered Parliament and continued to serve as Leader of the Opposition throughout the crisis over Lloyd George's <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Budget" title="People&#39;s Budget">1909 budget</a>, the narrow loss of two further General Elections in 1910, and the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Act_1911" title="Parliament Act 1911">Parliament Act 1911</a>. He resigned as party leader in 1911.
</p><p>Balfour returned as <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Admiralty" title="First Lord of the Admiralty">First Lord of the Admiralty</a> in Asquith's Coalition Government (1915–1916). In December 1916, he became foreign secretary in <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>'s coalition. He was frequently left out of the inner workings of foreign policy, although the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration" title="Balfour Declaration">Balfour Declaration</a> on a Jewish homeland bore his name. He continued to serve in senior positions throughout the 1920s, and died in 1930, aged 81, having spent a vast inherited fortune. He never married. Balfour trained as a philosopher&#160;&#8211;&#32;he originated an argument against believing that human reason could determine truth&#160;&#8211;&#32;and was seen as having a detached attitude to life, epitomised by a remark attributed to him: "Nothing matters very much and few things matter at all".
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Background_and_early_life">Background and early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Background and early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG/170px-Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="113" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG/255px-Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG/340px-Lord_Balfour%27s_childhood_home.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>Whittingehame House</figcaption></figure>
<p>Arthur Balfour was born at <a href="/wiki/Whittingehame_House" class="mw-redirect" title="Whittingehame House">Whittingehame House</a>, East Lothian, Scotland, the eldest son of <a href="/wiki/James_Maitland_Balfour" title="James Maitland Balfour">James Maitland Balfour</a> (1820–1856) and Lady Blanche Gascoyne-Cecil (1825–1872). His father was a Scottish MP, as was his grandfather <a href="/wiki/James_Balfour_(died_1845)" title="James Balfour (died 1845)">James</a>; his mother, a member of the <a href="/wiki/Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Marquess of Salisbury">Cecil family</a> descended from <a href="/wiki/Robert_Cecil,_1st_Earl_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Cecil, 1st Earl of Salisbury">Robert Cecil, 1st Earl of Salisbury</a>, was the daughter of the <a href="/wiki/James_Gascoyne-Cecil,_2nd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="James Gascoyne-Cecil, 2nd Marquess of Salisbury">2nd Marquess of Salisbury</a> and a sister of the <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 3rd Marquess of Salisbury">3rd Marquess</a>, the future prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911250_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911250-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> His godfather was the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington</a>, after whom he was named.<sup id="cite_ref-Tuch46_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tuch46-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He was the eldest son, third of eight children, and had four brothers and three sisters. Arthur Balfour was educated at Grange Preparatory School at <a href="/wiki/Hoddesdon,_Hertfordshire" class="mw-redirect" title="Hoddesdon, Hertfordshire">Hoddesdon, Hertfordshire</a> (1859–1861), and <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a> (1861–1866), where he studied with the influential master, <a href="/wiki/William_Johnson_Cory" title="William Johnson Cory">William Johnson Cory</a>. He then went up to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cambridge" title="University of Cambridge">University of Cambridge</a>, where he read <a href="/wiki/Moral_sciences" class="mw-redirect" title="Moral sciences">moral sciences</a> at <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge" title="Trinity College, Cambridge">Trinity College</a> (1866–1869),<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> graduating with a <a href="/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification" title="British undergraduate degree classification">second-class honours</a> degree.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His younger brother was the Cambridge embryologist <a href="/wiki/Francis_Maitland_Balfour" title="Francis Maitland Balfour">Francis Maitland Balfour</a> (1851–1882).<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Balfour met his cousin May Lyttelton in 1870 when she was 19. After her two previous serious suitors had died, Balfour is said to have declared his love for her in December 1874. She died of <a href="/wiki/Typhus" title="Typhus">typhus</a> on <a href="/wiki/Palm_Sunday" title="Palm Sunday">Palm Sunday</a>, 21 March 1875; Balfour arranged for an emerald ring to be buried in her coffin. Lavinia Talbot, May's older sister, believed that an engagement had been imminent, but her recollections of Balfour's distress (he was "staggered") were not written down until thirty years later.<sup id="cite_ref-Adams2007_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adams2007-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 29–33">&#58;&#8202;29–33&#8202;</span></sup> 
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/R._J._Q._Adams" title="R. J. Q. Adams">R. J. Q. Adams</a> points out that May's letters discuss her love life in detail, but contain no evidence that she was in love with Balfour, nor that he had spoken to her of marriage. He visited her only once during her serious three-month illness, and was soon accepting social invitations again within a month of her death. Adams suggests that, although he may simply have been too shy to express his feelings fully, Balfour may also have encouraged tales of his youthful tragedy as a convenient cover for his disinclination to marry; the matter cannot be conclusively proven.<sup id="cite_ref-Adams2007_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adams2007-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 29–33">&#58;&#8202;29–33&#8202;</span></sup> 
</p><p>In later years mediums claimed to pass on messages from her&#160;&#8211;&#32;see the "<a href="/wiki/Cross-Correspondences" title="Cross-Correspondences">Cross-Correspondences</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wilson_2011_530_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilson_2011_530-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour remained a lifelong bachelor. <a href="/wiki/Margot_Asquith,_Countess_of_Oxford_and_Asquith" class="mw-redirect" title="Margot Asquith, Countess of Oxford and Asquith">Margot Tennant</a> (later Margot Asquith) wished to marry him, but Balfour said: "No, that is not so. I rather think of having a career of my own."<sup id="cite_ref-Tuch46_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tuch46-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His household was maintained by his unmarried sister, Alice. In middle age, Balfour had a 40-year friendship with <a href="/wiki/Mary_Constance_Wyndham" title="Mary Constance Wyndham">Mary Charteris (née Wyndham), Lady Elcho</a>, later Countess of <a href="/wiki/Hugo_Charteris,_11th_Earl_of_Wemyss" title="Hugo Charteris, 11th Earl of Wemyss">Wemyss and March</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Although one biographer writes that "it is difficult to say how far the relationship went", her letters suggest they may have become lovers in 1887 and may have engaged in <a href="/wiki/Sado-masochistic" class="mw-redirect" title="Sado-masochistic">sado-masochism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Adams2007_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adams2007-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 47">&#58;&#8202;47&#8202;</span></sup> a claim echoed by <a href="/wiki/A._N._Wilson" title="A. N. Wilson">A. N. Wilson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Wilson_2011_530_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilson_2011_530-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Another biographer believes they had "no direct physical relationship", although he dismisses as "unlikely" suggestions that Balfour was homosexual, or, in view of a time during the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Boer War</a> when he was seen as he replied to a message while drying himself after his bath, <a href="/wiki/Lord_Beaverbrook" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Beaverbrook">Lord Beaverbrook</a>'s claim that he was "a <a href="/wiki/Hermaphrodite" title="Hermaphrodite">hermaphrodite</a>" whom no-one saw naked.<sup id="cite_ref-Mackay1985_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mackay1985-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour was a leading member of the social and intellectual group <a href="/wiki/The_Souls" title="The Souls">The Souls</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg/170px-Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="216" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg/255px-Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg/340px-Arthur_James_Balfour_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="428" data-file-height="543" /></a><figcaption>Balfour early in his career</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1874 Balfour was elected <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> Member of Parliament (MP) for <a href="/wiki/Hertford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Hertford (UK Parliament constituency)">Hertford</a> until 1885. From 1885 to 1906 he served as the Member of Parliament for <a href="/wiki/Manchester_East_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Manchester East (UK Parliament constituency)">Manchester East</a>. In spring 1878, he became <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Private_Secretary" title="Parliamentary Private Secretary">private secretary</a> to his uncle <a href="/wiki/Lord_Salisbury" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Salisbury">Lord Salisbury</a>. He accompanied Salisbury (then foreign secretary) to the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Berlin" title="Congress of Berlin">Congress of Berlin</a> and gained his first experience in international politics in connection with the settlement of the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_(1877%E2%80%931878)" title="Russo-Turkish War (1877–1878)">Russo-Turkish conflict</a>. At the same time he became known in the world of letters; the academic subtlety and literary achievement of his <i>Defence of Philosophic Doubt</i> (1879) suggested he might make a reputation as a philosopher.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911250–251_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911250–251-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour divided his time between politics and academic pursuits. Biographer Sydney Zebel suggested that Balfour continued to appear an amateur or dabbler in public affairs, devoid of ambition and indifferent to policy issues. However, in fact he actually made a dramatic transition to a deeply involved politician. His assets, according to Zebel, included a strong ambition that he kept hidden, shrewd political judgment, a knack for negotiation, a taste for intrigue, and care to avoid factionalism. Most importantly, he deepened his close ties with his uncle Lord Salisbury. He also maintained cordial relationships with Disraeli, Gladstone and other national leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 27">&#58;&#8202;27&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Released from his duties as private secretary by the <a href="/wiki/1880_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1880 United Kingdom general election">1880 general election</a>, he began to take more part in parliamentary affairs. He was for a time politically associated with <a href="/wiki/Lord_Randolph_Churchill" title="Lord Randolph Churchill">Lord Randolph Churchill</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sir_Henry_Drummond_Wolff" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Henry Drummond Wolff">Sir Henry Drummond Wolff</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Eldon_Gorst" title="John Eldon Gorst">John Gorst</a>. This quartet became known as the "<a href="/wiki/Fourth_Party" title="Fourth Party">Fourth Party</a>" and gained notoriety for leader Lord Randolph Churchill's free criticism of <a href="/wiki/Stafford_Northcote,_1st_Earl_of_Iddesleigh" title="Stafford Northcote, 1st Earl of Iddesleigh">Sir Stafford Northcote</a>, <a href="/wiki/R._A._Cross,_1st_Viscount_Cross" title="R. A. Cross, 1st Viscount Cross">Lord Cross</a> and other prominent members of the Conservative "old gang".<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 28–44">&#58;&#8202;28–44&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Green2006_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Green2006-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Service_in_Lord_Salisbury.27s_governments"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Service_in_Lord_Salisbury's_governments">Service in Lord Salisbury's governments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Service in Lord Salisbury&#039;s governments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gws_balfour_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gws_balfour_02.jpg/170px-Gws_balfour_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gws_balfour_02.jpg/255px-Gws_balfour_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gws_balfour_02.jpg/340px-Gws_balfour_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="712" data-file-height="1000" /></a><figcaption>Balfour <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1890</span></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Irish_Secretary">Irish Secretary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Irish Secretary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1885, Lord Salisbury appointed Balfour <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Local_Government_Board" title="President of the Local Government Board">President of the Local Government Board</a>; the following year he became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_for_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary for Scotland">Secretary for Scotland</a> with a seat in the cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> These offices, while offering few opportunities for distinction, were an apprenticeship. In early 1887, Sir <a href="/wiki/Michael_Hicks_Beach,_1st_Earl_St_Aldwyn" title="Michael Hicks Beach, 1st Earl St Aldwyn">Michael Hicks Beach</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a>, resigned because of illness and Salisbury appointed his nephew in his place. The selection was much criticized. It was received with contemptuous ridicule by the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Parliamentary_Party" title="Irish Parliamentary Party">Irish Nationalists</a>, for none suspected Balfour's immense strength of will, his debating power, his ability in attack and his still greater capacity to disregard criticism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>  Balfour surprised critics by ruthless enforcement of the <a href="/wiki/Criminal_Law_and_Procedure_(Ireland)_Act_1887" title="Criminal Law and Procedure (Ireland) Act 1887">Crimes Act</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Mitchelstown_Massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Mitchelstown Massacre">Mitchelstown Massacre</a> occurred on 9 September 1887, when <a href="/wiki/Royal_Irish_Constabulary" title="Royal Irish Constabulary">Royal Irish Constabulary</a> (RIC) members fired at a crowd protesting against the conviction under the Act of MP <a href="/wiki/William_O%27Brien" title="William O&#39;Brien">William O'Brien</a> and another man.<sup id="cite_ref-Comerford2010_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Comerford2010-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Three were killed by the RIC's gunfire. When Balfour defended the RIC in the Commons, O'Brien dubbed him "Bloody Balfour".<sup id="cite_ref-O&#39;Brien1976_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O&#39;Brien1976-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> His steady administration did much to dispel his reputation as a political lightweight.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Parliament he resisted overtures to the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Parliamentary_Party" title="Irish Parliamentary Party">Irish Parliamentary Party</a> on <a href="/wiki/Home_Rule" class="mw-redirect" title="Home Rule">Home Rule</a>, which he saw as an expression of superficial or false <a href="/wiki/Irish_nationalism" title="Irish nationalism">Irish nationalism</a>. Allied with <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Unionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Unionist">Liberal Unionists</a>, he encouraged <a href="/wiki/Unionists_(Ireland)" class="mw-redirect" title="Unionists (Ireland)">Unionist</a> activism in Ireland. Balfour also helped the poor by creating the <a href="/wiki/Congested_Districts_Board_for_Ireland" title="Congested Districts Board for Ireland">Congested Districts Board for Ireland</a> in 1890. Balfour downplayed the factor of Irish nationalism, arguing that the real issues were economic. Regarding ownership and control of the land, he believed that once violence was suppressed and land was sold to the tenants, Irish nationalism would no longer threaten the unity of the United Kingdom. The slogan "to kill home rule with kindness" characterized Balfour's new policy toward Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals had begun <a href="/wiki/Land_Acts_(Ireland)" title="Land Acts (Ireland)">land sales to Irish tenants</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Land_Law_(Ireland)_Act_1881" title="Land Law (Ireland) Act 1881">Land Law (Ireland) Act 1881</a> and this was expanded by the Conservatives in the <a href="/wiki/Purchase_of_Land_(Ireland)_Act_1885" title="Purchase of Land (Ireland) Act 1885">land purchase scheme of 1885</a>.  However the depression in agriculture kept prices low. Balfour's solution was to keep selling land and in 1887 lowering rents to match the lower prices, and protected more tenants against eviction by their landlords.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Balfour greatly expanded the land sales. They culminated in the final Unionist land purchase programme of 1903,  when Balfour was prime minister and <a href="/wiki/George_Wyndham" title="George Wyndham">George Wyndham</a> was the Irish secretary.  It encouraged landlords to sell by means of a 12% cash bonus. Tenants were encouraged to buy with a low interest rate, and payments drawn out over 68 years. In 1909, Liberal legislation required compulsory sales in certain cases. As the landlords sold out, they relocated to Britain, giving up their political power in Ireland. Tensions in the countryside dramatically declined as some 200,000 peasant proprietors owned about half the land in Ireland. After 1890 violence declined sharply, and as Balfour had hoped, Irish nationalism was a relatively minor factor. The situation suddenly was transformed when the <a href="/wiki/Easter_Rising" title="Easter Rising">Easter rebellion</a> of 1916 radicalised Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_roles">Leadership roles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Leadership roles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_James_Balfour,_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg/220px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="314" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg/330px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg/440px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Sir_Lawrence_Alma-Tadema.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3421" /></a><figcaption>Balfour in 1891, by <a href="/wiki/Lawrence_Alma-Tadema" title="Lawrence Alma-Tadema">Lawrence Alma-Tadema</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1886–1892 he became one of the most effective public speakers of the age. Impressive in matter rather than delivery, his speeches were logical and convincing, and delighted an ever-wider audience.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the death of <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Smith_(1825%E2%80%931891)" title="William Henry Smith (1825–1891)">W. H. Smith</a> in 1891, Balfour became <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury" title="First Lord of the Treasury">First Lord of the Treasury</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;the last in British history not to have been concurrently prime minister as well&#160;&#8211;&#32;and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a>. After the fall of the government in 1892 he spent three years in opposition. When the Conservatives returned to power, in coalition with the Liberal Unionists, in 1895, Balfour again became Leader of the House and First Lord of the Treasury. His management of the abortive education proposals of 1896 showed a disinclination for the drudgery of parliamentary management, yet he saw the passage of a bill providing Ireland with improved local government under the <a href="/wiki/Local_Government_(Ireland)_Act_1898" title="Local Government (Ireland) Act 1898">Local Government (Ireland) Act 1898</a> and joined in debates on foreign and domestic questions between 1895 and 1900.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the illness of Lord Salisbury in 1898, and again in Salisbury's absence abroad, Balfour was in charge of the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Office">Foreign Office</a>, and he conducted negotiations with Russia on the question of railways in North China. As a member of the cabinet responsible for the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Transvaal negotiations</a> in 1899, he bore his share of controversy and, when the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a> began disastrously, he was first to realise the need to use the country's full military strength. His leadership of the House was marked by firmness in the suppression of obstruction, yet there was a slight revival of the criticisms of 1896.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister">Prime minister<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Prime minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Balfour_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Balfour ministry">Balfour ministry</a></div>
<p>With Lord Salisbury's resignation on 11 July 1902, Balfour succeeded him as prime minister, with the approval of all the Unionist party. The new prime minister came into power practically at the same moment as the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_King_Edward_VII_and_Queen_Alexandra" class="mw-redirect" title="Coronation of King Edward VII and Queen Alexandra">coronation of King Edward VII and Queen Alexandra</a> and the end of the <a href="/wiki/South_African_War" class="mw-redirect" title="South African War">South African War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal party was still disorganised over the Boers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911252_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911252-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In foreign affairs, Balfour and his foreign secretary, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Petty-Fitzmaurice,_5th_Marquess_of_Lansdowne" title="Henry Petty-Fitzmaurice, 5th Marquess of Lansdowne">Lord Lansdowne</a>, improved relations with France, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Entente_Cordiale" title="Entente Cordiale">Entente Cordiale</a> of 1904. The period also saw the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War" title="Russo-Japanese War">Russo-Japanese War</a>, when Britain, an ally of the Japanese, came close to war with Russia after the <a href="/wiki/Dogger_Bank_incident" title="Dogger Bank incident">Dogger Bank incident</a>. On the whole, Balfour left the conduct of foreign policy to Lansdowne, being busy himself with domestic problems.<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour, who had known Zionist leader <a href="/wiki/Chaim_Weizmann" title="Chaim Weizmann">Chaim Weizmann</a> since 1906, opposed Russian mistreatment of Jews and increasingly supported Zionism as a programme for European Jews to settle in Palestine.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> However, in 1905 he supported the <a href="/wiki/Aliens_Act_1905" title="Aliens Act 1905">Aliens Act 1905</a>, one of whose main objectives was to control and restrict Jewish immigration from Eastern Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The budget was certain to show a surplus and taxation could be remitted. Yet as events proved, it was the budget that would sow dissension, override other legislative concerns and signal a new political movement. <a href="/wiki/Charles_Thomson_Ritchie" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles Thomson Ritchie">Charles Thomson Ritchie</a>'s remission of the shilling import-duty on corn led to <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a>'s crusade in favour of tariff reform. These were taxes on imported goods with <a href="/wiki/Trade_preference" title="Trade preference">trade preference</a> given to the Empire, to protect British industry from competition, strengthen the Empire in the face of growing German and American economic power, and provide revenue, other than raising taxes, for the social welfare legislation. As the session proceeded, the rift grew in the Unionist ranks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911252_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911252-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Tariff reform was popular with Unionist supporters, but the threat of higher prices for food imports made the policy an electoral albatross. Hoping to split the difference between the free traders and tariff reformers in his cabinet and party, Balfour favoured retaliatory tariffs to punish others who had tariffs against the British, in the hope of encouraging global free trade.
This was not sufficient for either the free traders or the extreme tariff reformers in government. With Balfour's agreement, Chamberlain resigned from the Cabinet in late 1903 to campaign for tariff reform. At the same time, Balfour tried to balance the two factions by accepting the resignation of three free-trading ministers, including Chancellor Ritchie, but the almost simultaneous resignation of the free-trader <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Cavendish,_8th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="Spencer Cavendish, 8th Duke of Devonshire">Duke of Devonshire</a> (who as Lord Hartington had been the Liberal Unionist leader of the 1880s) left Balfour's Cabinet weak. By 1905 few Unionist MPs were still free traders (<a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> crossed to the Liberals in 1904 when threatened with deselection at <a href="/wiki/Oldham_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Oldham (UK Parliament constituency)">Oldham</a>), but Balfour's act had drained his authority within the government.<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour resigned as prime minister in December 1905, hoping the Liberal leader <a href="/wiki/Henry_Campbell-Bannerman" title="Henry Campbell-Bannerman">Campbell-Bannerman</a> would be unable to form a strong government. This was dashed when Campbell-Bannerman faced down an attempt ("<a href="/wiki/The_Relugas_Compact" class="mw-redirect" title="The Relugas Compact">The Relugas Compact</a>") to "kick him upstairs" to the House of Lords. The Conservatives were defeated by the Liberals at the general election the following January (in terms of MPs, a Liberal landslide), with Balfour losing his seat at <a href="/wiki/Manchester_East_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Manchester East (UK Parliament constituency)">Manchester East</a> to <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Gardner_Horridge" title="Thomas Gardner Horridge">Thomas Gardner Horridge</a>, a solicitor and <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Counsel" title="King&#39;s Counsel">King's Counsel</a>. Only 157 Conservatives were returned to the Commons, at least two-thirds followers of Chamberlain, who chaired the Conservative MPs until Balfour won a by-election for a safe seat in the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="City of London (UK Parliament constituency)">City of London</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911254_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911254-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Achievements_and_mistakes">Achievements and mistakes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Achievements and mistakes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>According to historian <a href="/wiki/Robert_Ensor" title="Robert Ensor">Robert Ensor</a>, Balfour can be credited with achievement in five major areas:<sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 355">&#58;&#8202;355&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<ol><li>The <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1902" title="Education Act 1902">Education Act 1902</a> (and a similar measure for London in 1903);<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Land_Purchase_(Ireland)_Act_1903" class="mw-redirect" title="Land Purchase (Ireland) Act 1903">Land Purchase (Ireland) Act 1903</a>, which bought out the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Irish_people" title="Anglo-Irish people">Anglo-Irish</a> land owners;<sup id="cite_ref-Bull2016_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bull2016-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Licensing Act 1904;<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In military policy, the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Committee_of_Imperial_Defence" title="Committee of Imperial Defence">Committee of Imperial Defence</a> (1904) and support for Sir John Fisher's naval reforms.</li>
<li>In foreign policy, the Anglo-French Convention (1904), which formed the basis of the Entente with France.</li></ol>
<p>The Education Act lasted four decades and eventually was highly praised. Eugene Rasor states, "Balfour was credited and much praised from many perspectives with the success [of the 1902 education act]. His commitment to education was fundamental and strong."<sup id="cite_ref-Rasor1998_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rasor1998-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 20">&#58;&#8202;20&#8202;</span></sup> At the time it hurt Balfour because the Liberal party used it to rally their Noncomformist supporters. Ensor said the Act ranked:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>among the two or three greatest constructive measures of the twentieth century....[He did not write it] but no statesman less dominated than Balfour was by the concept of national efficiency would have taken it up and carried it through, since its cost on the side of votes was obvious and deterrent....Public money was thus made available for the first time to ensure properly paid teachers and a standardized level of efficiency for all children alike [including the Anglican and Catholic schools].<sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 355–56">&#58;&#8202;355–56&#8202;</span></sup>
</p></blockquote>
<p>For most of the 19th century, the very powerful political and economic position of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Ireland" title="Church of Ireland">Church of Ireland</a> (Anglican) landowners blocked the political aspirations of Irish nationalists, who by 1900 included both Catholic and Presbyterian elements. Balfour's solution was to buy them out, not by compulsion, but by offering the owners a full immediate payment and a 12% bonus on the sales price.  The British government purchased 13 million acres (53,000 km2) by 1920, and sold farms to the tenants at low payments spread over seven decades. It would cost money, but all sides proved amenable.<sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 358–60">&#58;&#8202;358–60&#8202;</span></sup>  Starting in 1923 the Irish government bought out most of the remaining landowners, and in 1933 diverted payments being made to the British treasury and used them for local improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-Lee1989_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lee1989-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour's introduction of Chinese coolie labour in South Africa enabled the Liberals to counterattack, charging that his measures amounted to "Chinese slavery".<sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 355, 376–78">&#58;&#8202;355,&#8202;376–78&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Likerwise Liberals energized the Nonconformists when they attacked Balfour's Licensing Act 1904 which paid pub owners to close down. In the long run it did reduce the great oversupply of pubs, while in the short run Balfour's party was hurt.<sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 360–61">&#58;&#8202;360–61&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Balfour failed to solve his greatest political challenge&#160;&#8211;&#32;the debate over tariffs that ripped his party apart. Chamberlain proposed to turn the Empire into a closed <a href="/wiki/Trade_bloc" title="Trade bloc">trade bloc</a> protected by high tariffs against imports from Germany and the United States. He argued that tariff reform would revive a flagging British economy, strengthen imperial ties with the dominions and the colonies, and produce a positive programme that would facilitate reelection. He was vehemently opposed by Conservative free traders who denounced the proposal as economically fallacious, and open to the charge of raising food prices in Britain. Balfour tried to forestall disruption by removing key ministers on each side, and offering a much narrower tariff programme. It was ingenious, but both sides rejected any compromise, and his party's chances for reelection were ruined.<sup id="cite_ref-Goodlad2010_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goodlad2010-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-PearceGoodlad2013_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PearceGoodlad2013-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 4–6">&#58;&#8202;4–6&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Historians generally praised Balfour's achievements in military and foreign policy. <a href="#CITEREFCannonCrowcroft2015">Cannon &amp; Crowcroft 2015</a> stress the importance of the Anglo‐French Entente of 1904, and the establishment of the Committee of Imperial Defence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams2002199_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams2002199-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Rasor points to twelve historians who have examined his key role in naval and military reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-Rasor1998_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rasor1998-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 39–40">&#58;&#8202;39–40&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ensor1936_31-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ensor1936-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 361–71">&#58;&#8202;361–71&#8202;</span></sup> However, there was little political payback at the time. The local Conservative campaigns in 1906 focused mostly on a few domestic issues.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Balfour gave strong support for <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher,_1st_Baron_Fisher" title="John Fisher, 1st Baron Fisher">Jackie Fisher</a>'s naval reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour created and chaired the <a href="/wiki/Committee_of_Imperial_Defence" title="Committee of Imperial Defence">Committee of Imperial Defence</a>, which provided better long-term coordinated planning between the Army and Navy.<sup id="cite_ref-Mackintosh1962_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mackintosh1962-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Austen_Chamberlain" title="Austen Chamberlain">Austen Chamberlain</a> said Britain would have been unprepared for the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a> without his Committee of Imperial Defence. He wrote, "It is impossible to overrate the services thus rendered by Balfour to the Country and Empire....[Without the CID] victory would have been impossible."<sup id="cite_ref-Young1975_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Young1975-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Historians also praised the Anglo-French Convention (1904), which formed the basis of the <a href="/wiki/Entente_Cordiale" title="Entente Cordiale">Entente Cordiale</a> with France that proved decisive in 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-MacMillan2013_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacMillan2013-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour may have been personally sympathetic to extending suffrage, with his brother <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Balfour,_2nd_Earl_of_Balfour" title="Gerald Balfour, 2nd Earl of Balfour">Gerald</a>, Conservative MP for <a href="/wiki/Leeds_Central_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Leeds Central (UK Parliament constituency)">Leeds Central</a> married to women's suffrage activist <a href="/wiki/Lady_Constance_Bulwer-Lytton" title="Lady Constance Bulwer-Lytton">Constance Lytton</a>'s sister <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Balfour,_Countess_of_Balfour" title="Elizabeth Balfour, Countess of Balfour">Betty</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> but he accepted the strength of the political opposition to women's suffrage, as shown in correspondence with <a href="/wiki/Christabel_Pankhurst" title="Christabel Pankhurst">Christabel Pankhurst</a>, a leader of the <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_Social_and_Political_Union" title="Women&#39;s Social and Political Union">WSPU</a>. Balfour argued that he was "not convinced the majority of women actually wanted the vote", in 1907. A rebuttal which meant extending the activist campaign for women's rights.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>  He was reminded by Lytton of a speech he made in 1892, namely that this question "will arise again, menacing and ripe for resolution", she asked him to meet WSPU leader, Christabel Pankhurst, after a series of hunger strikes and suffering by imprisoned suffragettes in 1907. Balfour refused on the grounds of her militancy.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Christabel pleaded direct to meet Balfour as Conservative party leader, on their policy manifesto for the General Election of 1909,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute"><span title="The material near this tag is possibly inaccurate or nonfactual. (August 2023)">dubious</span></a>&#32;<span class="metadata"> &#8211; <a href="/wiki/Talk:Arthur_Balfour#Dubious" title="Talk:Arthur Balfour">discuss</a></span></i>&#93;</sup> but he refused again as women's suffrage was "not a party question and his colleagues were divided on the matter".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> She tried and failed again to get his open support in parliament for women's cause in the 1910 private member's <a href="/wiki/Conciliation_Bills" title="Conciliation Bills">Conciliation Bill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He voted for the bill in the end but not for its progress to the Grand Committee, preventing it becoming law, and extending the activist campaigns as a result again.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The following year Lytton and Annie Kenney in person after another reading of the Bill, but again it was not prioritised as government business.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>  His sister-in-law Lady Betty Balfour spoke to Churchill that her brother was to speak for this policy, and also met the Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/H.H._Asquith" class="mw-redirect" title="H.H. Asquith">H.H. Asquith</a> in a 1911 delegation of the women's movements representing the Conservative and Unionist Women's Franchise Association<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> but it was not until 1918 that (some) women were given the right to vote in elections in the United Kingdom, despite a forty-year campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_47-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_career">Later career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Later career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:292px;max-width:292px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:145px;max-width:145px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:242px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_Balfour,_1908.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg/143px-Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg" decoding="async" width="143" height="242" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg/215px-Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg/286px-Arthur_Balfour%2C_1908.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="593" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Painting by <a href="/wiki/John_Singer_Sargent" title="John Singer Sargent">John Singer Sargent</a>, 1908</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:143px;max-width:143px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:242px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg/141px-Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg" decoding="async" width="141" height="243" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg/212px-Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg/282px-Arthur_Balfour_Vanity_Fair_27_January_1910.jpg 2x" data-file-width="340" data-file-height="585" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Balfour caricatured by <i><a href="/wiki/Vanity_Fair_(UK_magazine)" class="mw-redirect" title="Vanity Fair (UK magazine)">Vanity Fair</a></i>, 1910</div></div></div></div></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:British-Prime-Minister-Arthur-Balfour-with-Winston-Churchill.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/British-Prime-Minister-Arthur-Balfour-with-Winston-Churchill.jpg/220px-British-Prime-Minister-Arthur-Balfour-with-Winston-Churchill.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="276" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a5/British-Prime-Minister-Arthur-Balfour-with-Winston-Churchill.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="377" /></a><figcaption>Balfour and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> in 1911</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/1906_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1906 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1906</a> Balfour remained party leader, his position strengthened by Joseph Chamberlain's absence from the House of Commons after his stroke in July 1906, but he was unable to make much headway against the huge Liberal majority in the Commons. An early attempt to score a debating triumph over the government, made in Balfour's usual abstruse, theoretical style, saw Campbell-Bannerman respond with: "Enough of this foolery," to the delight of his supporters. Balfour made the controversial decision, with <a href="/wiki/Henry_Petty-Fitzmaurice,_5th_Marquess_of_Lansdowne" title="Henry Petty-Fitzmaurice, 5th Marquess of Lansdowne">Lord Lansdowne</a>, to use the heavily Unionist <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> as a check on the political programme and legislation of the Liberal party in the Commons. Legislation was vetoed or altered by amendments between 1906 and 1909, leading <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a> to remark that the Lords was "the right hon. Gentleman's poodle. It fetches and carries for him. It barks for him. It bites anybody that he sets it on to. And we are told that this is a great revising Chamber, the safeguard of liberty in the country."<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The issue was forced by the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberals</a> with Lloyd George's <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Budget" title="People&#39;s Budget">People's Budget</a>, provoking the constitutional crisis that led to the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Act_1911" title="Parliament Act 1911">Parliament Act 1911</a>, which limited the Lords to delaying bills for up to two years. After the Unionists lost the general elections of 1910 (despite softening the tariff reform policy with Balfour's promise of a referendum on food taxes), the Unionist peers split to allow the Parliament Act to pass the House of Lords, to prevent mass creation of Liberal peers by the new King, George V. The exhausted Balfour resigned as party leader after the crisis, and was succeeded in late 1911 by <a href="/wiki/Bonar_Law" title="Bonar Law">Bonar Law</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour remained important in the party, however, and when the Unionists joined <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">Asquith</a>'s coalition government in May 1915, Balfour succeeded Churchill as <a href="/wiki/List_of_the_First_Lords_of_the_Admiralty" class="mw-redirect" title="List of the First Lords of the Admiralty">First Lord of the Admiralty</a>. When Asquith's government collapsed in December 1916, Balfour, who seemed a potential successor to the premiership, became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">foreign secretary</a> in Lloyd George's new administration, but not in the small War Cabinet, and was frequently left out of inner workings of government. Balfour's service as foreign secretary was notable for the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Mission" title="Balfour Mission">Balfour Mission</a>, a crucial alliance-building visit to the US in April 1917, and the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration" title="Balfour Declaration">Balfour Declaration</a> of 1917, a letter to <a href="/wiki/Walter_Rothschild,_2nd_Baron_Rothschild" title="Walter Rothschild, 2nd Baron Rothschild">Lord Rothschild</a> affirming the government's support for the establishment of a "national home for the Jewish people" in <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a>, then part of the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour resigned as foreign secretary following the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Versailles Conference</a> in 1919, but continued in the government (and the Cabinet after normal peacetime political arrangements resumed) as <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a>. In 1921–22 he represented the British Empire at the <a href="/wiki/Washington_Naval_Conference" title="Washington Naval Conference">Washington Naval Conference</a> and during summer 1922 stood in for the foreign secretary, <a href="/wiki/George_Curzon,_1st_Marquess_Curzon_of_Kedleston" title="George Curzon, 1st Marquess Curzon of Kedleston">Lord Curzon</a>, who was ill. He put forward a proposal for the international settlement of war debts and reparations (the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Note" title="Balfour Note">Balfour Note</a>), but it was not accepted.<sup id="cite_ref-Zebel1973_15-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zebel1973-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 5 May 1922, Balfour was created <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Balfour" title="Earl of Balfour">Earl of Balfour</a> and Viscount Traprain, of <a href="/wiki/Whittingehame" title="Whittingehame">Whittingehame</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Haddington" class="mw-redirect" title="County of Haddington">county of Haddington</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In October 1922 he, with most of the Conservative leadership, resigned with Lloyd George's government following the <a href="/wiki/Carlton_Club_meeting" title="Carlton Club meeting">Carlton Club meeting</a>, a Conservative back-bench revolt against continuance of the coalition. <a href="/wiki/Bonar_Law" title="Bonar Law">Bonar Law</a> became prime minister.  Like many Coalition leaders, he did not hold office in the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_government,_1922%E2%80%931924" title="Conservative government, 1922–1924">Conservative governments of 1922–1924</a>, but as an elder statesman, he was consulted by the <a href="/wiki/George_V_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George V of the United Kingdom">King</a> in the choice of <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> as Bonar Law's successor as Conservative leader in May 1923. His advice was strongly in favour of Baldwin, ostensibly due to Baldwin's being an MP but in reality motivated by his personal dislike of Curzon. Later that evening, he met a mutual friend who asked 'Will dear George be chosen?' to which he replied with "feline Balfourian satisfaction," "No, dear George will not." His hostess replied, "Oh, I am so sorry to hear that. He will be terribly disappointed." Balfour retorted, "Oh, I don't know. After all, even if he has lost the hope of glory he still possesses the <a href="/wiki/Grace_Curzon,_Marchioness_Curzon_of_Kedleston" title="Grace Curzon, Marchioness Curzon of Kedleston">means of Grace</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour was not initially included in <a href="/wiki/Second_Baldwin_ministry" title="Second Baldwin ministry">Baldwin's second government</a> in 1924, but in 1925, he returned to the Cabinet, in place of the late Lord Curzon as <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a>, until the government ended in 1929. With 28 years of government service, Balfour had one of the longest ministerial careers in modern British politics, second only to Winston Churchill.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_years">Last years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Last years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann,_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs,_1925.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg/220px-Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg/330px-Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg/440px-Lord_Balfour_in_Biyamina_with_Vera_and_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Nachum_Sokolov_and_otehrs%2C_1925.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4972" data-file-height="3579" /></a><figcaption>Balfour in <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine" title="Mandatory Palestine">Mandatory Palestine</a> with <a href="/wiki/Vera_Weizmann" title="Vera Weizmann">Vera</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chaim_Weizmann" title="Chaim Weizmann">Chaim Weizmann</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nahum_Sokolow" title="Nahum Sokolow">Nahum Sokolow</a> and others in 1925</figcaption></figure>
<p>Lord Balfour had generally good health until 1928 and remained until then a regular tennis player. Four years previously he had been the first president of the International Lawn Tennis Club of Great Britain. At the end of 1928, most of his teeth were removed and he suffered the unremitting circulatory trouble which ended his life. Before that, he had suffered occasional <a href="/wiki/Phlebitis" title="Phlebitis">phlebitis</a> and, by late 1929, he was immobilised by it.  Following a visit from Chaim Weizmann, Balfour died at his brother Gerald's home, Fishers Hill House in Hook Heath, <a href="/wiki/Woking" title="Woking">Woking</a>, where he had lived since January 1929, on 19 March 1930. At his request a public funeral was declined, and he was buried on 22 March beside members of his family at <a href="/wiki/Whittingehame" title="Whittingehame">Whittingehame</a> in a <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Scotland" title="Church of Scotland">Church of Scotland</a> service although he also belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>. By <a href="/wiki/Special_remainder" class="mw-redirect" title="Special remainder">special remainder</a>, his title passed to his brother Gerald.<sup id="cite_ref-odnb_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-odnb-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His obituaries in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Herald_(UK_newspaper)" class="mw-redirect" title="Daily Herald (UK newspaper)">Daily Herald</a></i> did not mention the declaration for which he is most famous outside Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality">Personality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Personality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_James_Balfour,_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg/170px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg/255px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg/340px-Arthur_James_Balfour%2C_1st_Earl_of_Balfour_by_Philip_Alexius_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3396" /></a><figcaption>Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Philip_de_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3" title="Philip de László">Philip de László</a>, 1908</figcaption></figure>
<p>Early in Balfour's career he was thought to be merely amusing himself with politics, and it was regarded as doubtful whether his health could withstand the severity of English winters. He was considered a dilettante by his colleagues; regardless, Lord Salisbury gave increasingly powerful posts in his government to his nephew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911251-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Beatrice_Webb" title="Beatrice Webb">Beatrice Webb</a> wrote in her diary:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>A man of extraordinary grace of mind and body, delighting in all that is beautiful and distinguished––music, literature, philosophy, religious feeling and moral disinterestedness, aloof from all the greed and crying of common human nature. But a strange paradox as Prime Minister of a great empire! I doubt whether even foreign affairs interest him. For all economic and social questions I gather he has an utter loathing, while the machinery of government and administration would seem to him a disagreeable irrelevance.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Balfour developed a manner known to friends as the <i>Balfourian manner</i>. <a href="/wiki/Harold_Begbie" title="Harold Begbie">Harold Begbie</a>, a journalist, attacked him for what Begbie considered Balfour's self-obsession:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>This Balfourian manner...an attitude of mind—an attitude of convinced superiority which insists in the first place on complete detachment from the enthusiasms of the human race, and in the second place on keeping the vulgar world at arm's length....To Mr. Arthur Balfour this studied attitude of aloofness has been fatal, both to his character and to his career. He has said nothing, written nothing, done nothing, which lives in the heart of his countrymen....the charming, gracious, and cultured Mr. Balfour is the most egotistical of men, and a man who would make almost any sacrifice to remain in office.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>However, Graham Goodlad argued to the contrary:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Balfour's air of detachment was a pose. He was sincere in his conservatism, mistrusting radical political and social change and believing deeply in the Union with Ireland, the Empire and the superiority of the British race....Those who dismissed him as a languid dilettante were wide of the mark. As Chief Secretary for Ireland from 1887 to 1891 he manifested an unflinching commitment to the maintenance of British authority in the face of popular protest. He combined a strong emphasis on law and order with measures aimed at reforming the landowning system and developing Ireland's backward rural economy.<sup id="cite_ref-Goodlad2010_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goodlad2010-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Churchill compared Balfour to <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a>: "The difference between Balfour and Asquith is that Arthur is wicked and moral, while Asquith is good and immoral." Balfour said of himself, "I am more or less happy when being praised, not very comfortable when being abused, but I have moments of uneasiness when being explained."<sup id="cite_ref-gov._Hist_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gov._Hist-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour was interested in the study of <a href="/wiki/Dialect" title="Dialect">dialects</a> and donated money to <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Wright_(linguist)" title="Joseph Wright (linguist)">Joseph Wright</a>'s work on <i><a href="/wiki/The_English_Dialect_Dictionary" title="The English Dialect Dictionary">The English Dialect Dictionary</a></i>.  Wright wrote in the preface to the first volume that the project would have been "in vain" had he not received the donation from Balfour.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour was into the 1920s a keen player both of <a href="/wiki/Lawn_tennis" class="mw-redirect" title="Lawn tennis">lawn tennis</a> and of golf.
