In the latter sport, he had a <a href="/wiki/Handicap_(golf)" title="Handicap (golf)">handicap</a> of eight at the time he was Prime Minister, he won the Parliamentary Handicap between members of parliament in 1894, 1897 and 1910, and served as captain both of <a href="/wiki/The_Royal_and_Ancient_Golf_Club_of_St_Andrews" title="The Royal and Ancient Golf Club of St Andrews">The Royal and Ancient Golf Club of St Andrews</a> in 1894 and the newly founded Lye club in 1895.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>  Balfour was a patron of <a href="/wiki/Manchester_City_F.C." title="Manchester City F.C.">Manchester City F.C.</a><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> He was also a keen motorist and received, as an 80th birthday present, a Rolls-Royce from both Houses of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-odnb_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-odnb-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings_and_academic_achievements">Writings and academic achievements</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Writings and academic achievements">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>As a philosopher, Balfour formulated the basis for the <a href="/wiki/Evolutionary_argument_against_naturalism" title="Evolutionary argument against naturalism">evolutionary argument against naturalism</a>. Balfour argued the Darwinian premise of selection for reproductive fitness cast doubt on scientific naturalism, because human cognitive facilities that would accurately perceive truth could be less advantageous than adaptation for evolutionarily useful illusions.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As he says:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[There is] no distinction to be drawn between the development of reason and that of any other faculty, physiological or psychical, by which the interests of the individual or the race are promoted. From the humblest form of nervous irritation at the one end of the scale, to the reasoning capacity of the most advanced races at the other, everything without exception (sensation, instinct, desire, volition) has been produced directly or indirectly, by natural causes acting for the most part on strictly utilitarian principles. Convenience, not knowledge, therefore, has been the main end to which this process has tended.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Arthur Balfour<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBalfour191568_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBalfour191568-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>He was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Society_for_Psychical_Research" title="Society for Psychical Research">Society for Psychical Research</a>, a society studying <a href="/wiki/Psychic" title="Psychic">psychic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paranormal_phenomena" class="mw-redirect" title="Paranormal phenomena">paranormal phenomena</a>, and was its president from 1892 to 1894.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> In 1914, he delivered the <a href="/wiki/Gifford_Lectures" title="Gifford Lectures">Gifford Lectures</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Glasgow" title="University of Glasgow">University of Glasgow</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> which formed the basis for his book <i>Theism and Humanism</i> (1915).<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Views_on_race">Views on race</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Views on race">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1906, during a House of Commons debate, Balfour argued that the disenfranchisement of the blacks in South Africa was not immoral. He said:<sup id="cite_ref-PS_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PS-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>We have to face the facts. Men are not born equal, the white and black races are not born with equal capacities: they are born with different capacities which education cannot and will not change.</p></blockquote>
<p>Political scholar <a href="/wiki/Yousef_Munayyer" title="Yousef Munayyer">Yousef Munayyer</a> has claimed that Arthur Balfour's anti-Semitism played a role in the issuance of the Balfour Declaration, citing Balfour's presiding over, as prime minister, the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Aliens_Act_1905" title="Aliens Act 1905">Aliens Act 1905</a> that mainly aimed to restrict Jewish immigration to Britain from Eastern Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-PS_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PS-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Balfour had written in 1919, in his introduction to <a href="/wiki/Nahum_Sokolow" title="Nahum Sokolow">Nahum Sokolow</a>'s <i>History of Zionism</i>, that the Zionist movement would:<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>mitigate the age-long miseries created for Western civilization by the presence in its midst of a Body [the Jews] which it too long regarded as alien and even hostile, but which it was equally unable to expel or to absorb.</p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Artistic">Artistic</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Artistic">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg/170px-Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg/255px-Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg/340px-Arthur_James_Balfour.jpg 2x" data-file-width="573" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Walter_Stoneman" title="Walter Stoneman">Walter Stoneman</a>, 1921</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the First World War, when there was controversy over the style of headstone proposed for use on British war graves being taken on by the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_War_Graves_Commission" title="Commonwealth War Graves Commission">Imperial War Graves Commission</a>, Balfour submitted a design for a cruciform headstone.<sup id="cite_ref-Longworth1985_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth1985-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> At an exhibition in August 1919, it drew many criticisms; the commission's principal architect, <a href="/wiki/John_James_Burnet" title="John James Burnet">Sir John Burnet</a> said that Balfour's cross, if used in large numbers in cemeteries, would create a criss-cross visual effect, destroying any sense of "restful diginity"; <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Lutyens" title="Edwin Lutyens">Edwin Lutyens</a> called it "extraordinarily ugly", and its shape was variously described as resembling a shooting target or bottle.<sup id="cite_ref-Longworth1985_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth1985-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> His design was not accepted but the Commission offered him a second chance to submit another design which he did not take up, having been refused once.<sup id="cite_ref-Longworth1985_68-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth1985-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 49">&#58;&#8202;49&#8202;</span></sup> After a further exhibition in the House of Commons, the "Balfour cross" was ultimately rejected in favour of the standard headstone the Commission permanently adopted because the latter offered more space for inscriptions and service emblems.<sup id="cite_ref-Longworth1985_68-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth1985-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 50">&#58;&#8202;50&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture">Popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Balfour occasionally appears in popular culture.<sup id="cite_ref-Rasor1998_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rasor1998-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Balfour was the subject of two parody novels based on <a href="/wiki/Alice_in_Wonderland" class="mw-redirect" title="Alice in Wonderland">Alice in Wonderland</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Clara_in_Blunderland" title="Clara in Blunderland">Clara in Blunderland</a></i> (1902) and <i><a href="/wiki/Lost_in_Blunderland" title="Lost in Blunderland">Lost in Blunderland</a></i> (1903), which appeared under the pseudonym Caroline Lewis; one of the co-authors was <a href="/wiki/Harold_Begbie" title="Harold Begbie">Harold Begbie</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The character Arthur Balfour plays a supporting, off-screen role in <i><a href="/wiki/Upstairs,_Downstairs_(1971_TV_series)" title="Upstairs, Downstairs (1971 TV series)">Upstairs, Downstairs</a></i>, promoting the family patriarch, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Bellamy_(Upstairs,_Downstairs)" title="Richard Bellamy (Upstairs, Downstairs)">Richard Bellamy</a>, to the position of Civil Lord of the Admiralty.</li>
<li>Balfour was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Adrian_Ropes" title="Adrian Ropes">Adrian Ropes</a> in the 1974 <a href="/wiki/Thames_TV" class="mw-redirect" title="Thames TV">Thames TV</a> production <i><a href="/wiki/Jennie:_Lady_Randolph_Churchill" title="Jennie: Lady Randolph Churchill">Jennie: Lady Randolph Churchill</a></i>.</li>
<li>Balfour was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Lyndon_Brook" title="Lyndon Brook">Lyndon Brook</a> in the 1975 <a href="/wiki/Associated_Television" title="Associated Television">ATV</a> production <i><a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Seventh" title="Edward the Seventh">Edward the Seventh</a></i>.</li>
<li>A fictionalised version of Arthur Balfour (identified as "Mr. Balfour") appears as British prime minister in the science fiction romance <i><a href="/wiki/The_Angel_of_the_Revolution" title="The Angel of the Revolution">The Angel of the Revolution</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/George_Griffith" title="George Griffith">George Griffith</a>, published in 1893 (when Balfour was still in opposition) but set in an imagined near future of 1903–1905.</li>
<li>The indecisive Balfour (identified as "Halfan Halfour") appears in "Ministers of Grace", a satirical short story by <a href="/wiki/Saki" title="Saki">Saki</a> in which he, and other leading politicians including Quinston, are changed into animals appropriate to their characters.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Balfour's premiership from July 1902 to December 1905 is unusual among modern prime ministers because it did not mark the culmination of his political career. Instead, it was merely an episode in a long life of public service, and he quickly recovered from the setbacks of his chequered premiership. He continued to serve in government for nearly a quarter of a century after leaving 10 Downing Street, despite being forced from the leadership of his party.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balfour's reputation among historians is mixed. There is agreement about his achievements, as represented by <a href="/wiki/G._M._Trevelyan" title="G. M. Trevelyan">G. M. Trevelyan</a>:
</p>
<dl><dd>As the prime author of the Education Act, the Licensing Act, Irish Land Purchase and the Committee of Imperial Defence, Balfour has a strong claim to be numbered among the successful Prime Ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>But Trevelyan admits that, "owing to the portentous character of the electoral catastrophe of 1906 that claim is not always been allowed; yet Balfour had done great things on his own initiative and by his own strength of character."<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>  John L. Gordon pays more attention to the defeats he suffered, stating:
</p>
<dl><dd>Although Balfour's achievements during his brief prime ministry are noteworthy... he is usually seen as an ineffective leader. He was unable to prevent a split in his party over trade policy, and the Unionist-Conservatives suffered a massive defeat in the election of January 1906. Failing to lead his party to victory in the two general elections of 1910, he resigned as leader in 1911.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Memorials">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Balfour_stamp.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Balfour_stamp.jpg/220px-Balfour_stamp.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f0/Balfour_stamp.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="236" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption>1967 Israel stamp commemorating the 50th anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration" title="Balfour Declaration">Balfour Declaration</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Balfouria" title="Balfouria">Balfouria</a>, a <i><a href="/wiki/Moshav" title="Moshav">moshav</a></i> in northern Israel, along with many streets in Israel, are named after him. The town of <a href="/wiki/Balfour,_Mpumalanga" title="Balfour, Mpumalanga">Balfour</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mpumalanga" title="Mpumalanga">Mpumalanga</a>, South Africa, was named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-Raper1989_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Raper1989-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
A portrait of Balfour by <a href="/wiki/Philip_de_Laszlo" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip de Laszlo">Philip de Laszlo</a> is in the collection of <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge" title="Trinity College, Cambridge">Trinity College, Cambridge</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Lord Balfour Hotel, an <a href="/wiki/Art_Deco" title="Art Deco">Art Deco</a> hotel on <a href="/wiki/Ocean_Drive_(South_Beach)" title="Ocean Drive (South Beach)">Ocean Drive</a> in the <a href="/wiki/South_Beach" title="South Beach">South Beach</a> neighbourhood of <a href="/wiki/Miami_Beach,_Florida" title="Miami Beach, Florida">Miami Beach, Florida</a>, is named after him.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours_and_decorations">Honours and decorations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Balfour&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Honours and decorations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>His appointment as a <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Lieutenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy Lieutenant">Deputy Lieutenant</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ross-shire" title="Ross-shire">Ross-shire</a> on 10 September 1880 gave him the <a href="/wiki/List_of_post-nominal_letters_(United_Kingdom)" title="List of post-nominal letters (United Kingdom)">post-nominal letters</a> "DL".<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>He was sworn of the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council of the United Kingdom</a> in 1885, giving him the style "<a href="/wiki/The_Right_Honourable" title="The Right Honourable">The Right Honourable</a>" and after ennoblement the <a href="/wiki/List_of_post-nominal_letters_(United_Kingdom)" title="List of post-nominal letters (United Kingdom)">post-nominal letters</a> "PC" for life.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>On 3 June 1916 he was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Merit" title="Order of Merit">Order of Merit</a>, giving him the <a href="/wiki/List_of_post-nominal_letters_(United_Kingdom)" title="List of post-nominal letters (United Kingdom)">post-nominal letters</a> "OM" for life.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1919 he was elected <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_(education)" title="Chancellor (education)">Chancellor</a> of his old university, Cambridge, in succession to his brother-in-law, <a href="/wiki/John_William_Strutt,_3rd_Baron_Rayleigh" title="John William Strutt, 3rd Baron Rayleigh">Lord Rayleigh</a>.</li>
<li>He was made a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Knight Companion of the Order of the Garter</a> on 3 March 1922, becoming Sir Arthur Balfour and giving him the <a href="/wiki/List_of_post-nominal_letters_(United_Kingdom)" title="List of post-nominal letters (United Kingdom)">post-nominal letters</a> "KG" for life.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>On 5 May 1922 Balfour was raised to the peerage as <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Balfour" title="Earl of Balfour">Earl of Balfour</a> and Viscount Traprain, of Whittingehame, in the county of Haddington. This allowed him to sit in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>He was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Cross_of_Liberty_(Estonia)" title="Cross of Liberty (Estonia)">Estonian Cross of Liberty</a> (conferred between 1919 and 1925), third grade, first class, for Civilian Service.</li></ul>
<p>He was given the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_City" title="Freedom of the City">Freedom of the City/Freedom of the Borough</a> of the following:
</p>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland"><img alt="Scotland" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/23px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/35px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/46px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="600" /></a></span></span> 28 September 1899: <a href="/wiki/Dundee" title="Dundee">Dundee</a></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/East_Lothian" title="East Lothian"><img alt="East Lothian" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg/23px-Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg/35px-Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg/46px-Flag_of_East_Lothian.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="540" /></a></span></span> 20 September 1902: <a href="/wiki/Haddington,_East_Lothian" title="Haddington, East Lothian">Haddington, East Lothian</a><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh"><img alt="Edinburgh" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg/23px-Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg/35px-Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg/46px-Flag_of_Edinburgh.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="426" data-file-height="255" /></a></span></span> 19 October 1905: <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a></li></ul>
